========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, thanks for seeing me. I guess what brings me in is... I just keep getting stuck on this sagging, uneven look around my jaw, mostly on one side of my face. I really started fixating on it after seeing myself in all these family photos we took last month. We were up at the lake for my wife's family reunion, which, honestly, is always a bit of a production. Her brother insists on renting this massive pontoon boat every year, even though half of us get seasick, and the rental prices have just gone through the roof lately. I'm a personal financial advisor, so I can't help but look at what they're charging for those things and just cringe. It's a terrible return on investment, honestly, you're just burning cash. 

But anyway, in the pictures from the trip, my jaw just looks completely off. My wife says she barely notices anything, and my clients at work say they don't see it either—well, except for this one client who talks entirely too much about his golf swing anyway, but he didn't mention my face, he just talks a lot. I had to spend an hour yesterday just trying to talk him out of moving his entire retirement into crypto. It's wild what people want to do with their money. But yeah, the pictures... I just can't stop seeing how lopsided my face looks.

I mean, I'm not really sure how to quantify it. It bothers me, sure, but it's not like I'm falling apart over it or anything. It's just... annoying, you know? 

During the day, I'm mostly just stationed at my desk anyway. Well, actually, I just upgraded to one of those convertible standing desks last week because sitting for eight, nine hours a day was just absolutely destroying my lower back. My chiropractor recommended it. Dr. Evans. Great guy, really knows his stuff with spinal alignment, but man, he is an absolute disaster when it comes to his own retirement planning. We were looking at his portfolio on Tuesday, and he is way too heavy in municipal bonds for a guy who's only forty-two. I told him, look, with inflation doing what it's doing right now, you're essentially just locking in a loss. You gotta have some growth in there or you'll be working until you're eighty. 

But, uh... what were we talking about? Oh, right, my face. Yeah, sometimes my computer monitor goes to sleep and I'll catch my reflection in the black screen, and I'll just see that lopsidedness on my jaw. But I don't know exactly how much it bothers me overall. Just enough that I notice it and wish it looked normal, I guess.

Well, I mean, I still get through my work. I'm not calling in sick or anything. But I guess... I have been a little slower getting out the door in the mornings, especially if I have client meetings. You know, trying to make sure I'm angled right in the mirror before I leave. And then during the actual appointments, I feel like I'm maybe a little less present? Like I'm sitting there across from a client, and instead of fully focusing on their asset allocation, I'm wondering if the lighting in the conference room is highlighting the unevenness. 

Our office lighting is terrible, by the way. They put in these harsh LEDs last year to save on energy costs. I actually ran the numbers for the building manager—because I couldn't help myself—and I showed him that the payback period on those specific fixtures is like twelve years. By the time they actually see a return on investment, the technology will be obsolete anyway. Plus, everyone looks like a ghost under them. It's just bad business. If a client comes in to hand over half a million dollars for you to manage, they want to sit in a room that feels warm and secure, not like a hospital cafeteria, right? 

But yeah, the jaw thing. It's definitely making me a little more hesitant socially. My wife loves going to these local art gallery openings—she's really into watercolors right now—and I've actually skipped the last few. I just told her I had to catch up on some quarterly reports, but honestly, I just didn't want to be seen. And I definitely didn't want to end up in the background of anyone's photos. Those gallery people are always taking pictures for their Instagrams, and I just... I didn't want to deal with seeing my face like that again.

My chiropractor recommended it. Dr. Evans. Great guy, really knows his stuff with spinal alignment, but man, he is an absolute disaster when it comes to his own retirement planning. We were looking at his portfolio on Tuesday, and he is way too heavy in municipal bonds for a guy who's only forty-two. I told him, look, with inflation doing what it's doing right now, you're essentially just locking in a loss. Yeah, sometimes my computer monitor goes to sleep and I'll catch my reflection in the black screen, and I'll just see that lopsidedness on my jaw. Just enough that I notice it and wish it looked normal, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if this concern about your jaw's appearance affects your mood, daily routine, or how you interact with others?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean, I still get through my work. I'm not calling in sick or anything. But I guess... I have been a little slower getting out the door in the mornings, especially if I have client meetings. You know, trying to make sure I'm angled right in the mirror before I leave. And then during the actual appointments, I feel like I'm maybe a little less present? Like I'm sitting there across from a client, and instead of fully focusing on their asset allocation, I'm wondering if the lighting in the conference room is highlighting the unevenness. \n\nOur office lighting is terrible, by the way. They put in these harsh LEDs last year to save on energy costs. I actually ran the numbers for the building manager—because I couldn't help myself—and I showed him that the payback period on those specific fixtures is like twelve years. By the time they actually see a return on investment, the technology will be obsolete anyway. Plus, everyone looks like a ghost under them. It's just bad business. If a client comes in to hand over half a million dollars for you to manage, they want to sit in a room that feels warm and secure, not like a hospital cafeteria, right? \n\nBut yeah, the jaw thing. It's definitely making me a little more hesitant socially. My wife loves going to these local art gallery openings—she's really into watercolors right now—and I've actually skipped the last few. I just told her I had to catch up on some quarterly reports, but honestly, I just didn't want to be seen. And I definitely didn't want to end up in the background of anyone's photos. Those gallery people are always taking pictures for their Instagrams, and I just... I didn't want to deal with seeing my face like that again.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you ever feel significant anxiety or distress when you think that others might notice the appearance of your jaw?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. I don't know if I'd call it significant anxiety. I mean, I guess I get a little worked up about it, but it's not like a panic attack or anything. It's more just... a really uncomfortable feeling, like a tightness in my chest when I think someone is staring right at the lopsided part.

Turned out it was just severe heartburn from this spicy food truck he went to for lunch. Those food trucks are everywhere now, especially downtown near the financial district. They charge like eighteen dollars for a taco plate, which is just robbery when you consider their overhead is basically gas and a parking permit. I was talking to one of the owners once, and he was telling me about his profit margins, and I was just floored. I mean, if you manage your supply chain right, a food truck is basically a license to print money. But anyway, my client was fine, just needed an antacid. 

But yeah, the anxiety... I don't know if it's significant. I just don't like the idea of people staring at my jaw and thinking it looks weird. It's just unpleasant, you know?

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I suppose I do a bit of that. Like, right before a client comes in, I'll definitely head to the bathroom mirror, or I'll pull out my phone camera and check my reflection a few times. 

The cameras on these new phones are just ridiculous, by the way. They have, what, three lenses on the back now? My nephew was trying to explain the optical zoom to me at Thanksgiving, but honestly, who needs that much resolution? He just bought the newest model, which is wild to me because he's still paying off his student loans at like seven percent interest. I tried to tell him, I said, "Buddy, if you took that twelve hundred bucks and put it in a basic S&P 500 index fund, with compound interest over thirty years..." but kids these days just want the instant gratification. They don't think long-term at all. 

It's exactly the same with the younger advisors at my firm, honestly. They all want to push crypto and high-risk tech stocks because they think they're going to beat the market overnight. But... actually, speaking of the younger guys, I do catch myself looking at them a lot. Like, comparing my face to theirs. Or even when I do go to those gallery events with my wife, I'll realize I'm just standing there comparing my jaw to the other guys in the room instead of actually focusing on whatever conversation I'm supposed to be having. 

And then, yeah, I'll find myself messing with my shirt collar, or tilting my head to find a better angle. I even adjust the blinds in my office to change the lighting before someone walks in. Just trying to make sure it doesn't cast a weird shadow on my jaw, you know?

I just can't stop seeing how lopsided my face looks.\n\nDoctor: How much does the appearance of your jaw bother you when you think about it during the day?\n\nPatient: I mean, I'm not really sure how to quantify it. It bothers me, sure, but it's not like I'm falling apart over it or anything. It's just... annoying, you know? \n\nDuring the day, I'm mostly just stationed at my desk anyway. Well, actually, I just upgraded to one of those convertible standing desks last week because sitting for eight, nine hours a day was just absolutely destroying my lower back. My chiropractor recommended it. Dr. Evans. Great guy, really knows his stuff with spinal alignment, but man, he is an absolute disaster when it comes to his own retirement planning. We were looking at his portfolio on Tuesday, and he is way too heavy in municipal bonds for a guy who's only forty-two. I told him, look, with inflation doing what it's doing right now, you're essentially just locking in a loss. You gotta have some growth in there or you'll be working until you're eighty. \n\nBut, uh... what were we talking about? Oh, right, my face. Yeah, sometimes my computer monitor goes to sleep and I'll catch my reflection in the black screen, and I'll just see that lopsidedness on my jaw. But I don't know exactly how much it bothers me overall. Just enough that I notice it and wish it looked normal, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if this concern about your jaw's appearance affects your mood, daily routine, or how you interact with others?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean, I still get through my work. I'm not calling in sick or anything. But I guess... I have been a little slower getting out the door in the mornings, especially if I have client meetings. You know, trying to make sure I'm angled right in the mirror before I leave. And then during the actual appointments, I feel like I'm maybe a little less present? Like I'm sitting there across from a client, and instead of fully focusing on their asset allocation, I'm wondering if the lighting in the conference room is highlighting the unevenness. \n\nOur office lighting is terrible, by the way. They put in these harsh LEDs last year to save on energy costs. I actually ran the numbers for the building manager—because I couldn't help myself—and I showed him that the payback period on those specific fixtures is like twelve years. By the time they actually see a return on investment, the technology will be obsolete anyway. Plus, everyone looks like a ghost under them. It's just bad business. If a client comes in to hand over half a million dollars for you to manage, they want to sit in a room that feels warm and secure, not like a hospital cafeteria, right? \n\nBut yeah, the jaw thing. It's definitely making me a little more hesitant socially. My wife loves going to these local art gallery openings—she's really into watercolors right now—and I've actually skipped the last few. I just told her I had to catch up on some quarterly reports, but honestly, I just didn't want to be seen. And I definitely didn't want to end up in the background of anyone's photos. Those gallery people are always taking pictures for their Instagrams, and I just... I didn't want to deal with seeing my face like that again.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel significant anxiety or distress when you think that others might notice the appearance of your jaw?\n\nPatient: I don't know if I'd call it significant anxiety. I mean, I guess I get a little worked up about it, but it's not like a panic attack or anything. It's more just... a really uncomfortable feeling, like a tightness in my chest when I think someone is staring right at the lopsided part. \n\nActually, speaking of tightness in the chest, I had this client last month who thought he was having a heart attack right in my office. Turned out it was just severe heartburn from this spicy food truck he went to for lunch. Those food trucks are everywhere now, especially downtown near the financial district. They charge like eighteen dollars for a taco plate, which is just robbery when you consider their overhead is basically gas and a parking permit. I was talking to one of the owners once, and he was telling me about his profit margins, and I was just floored. I mean, if you manage your supply chain right, a food truck is basically a license to print money. But anyway, my client was fine, just needed an antacid. \n\nBut yeah, the anxiety... I don't know if it's significant. I just don't like the idea of people staring at my jaw and thinking it looks weird. It's just unpleasant, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain behaviors, like checking your reflection, adjusting your posture, or seeking reassurance about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I suppose I do a bit of that. Like, right before a client comes in, I'll definitely head to the bathroom mirror, or I'll pull out my phone camera and check my reflection a few times. \n\nThe cameras on these new phones are just ridiculous, by the way. They have, what, three lenses on the back now? My nephew was trying to explain the optical zoom to me at Thanksgiving, but honestly, who needs that much resolution? He just bought the newest model, which is wild to me because he's still paying off his student loans at like seven percent interest. I tried to tell him, I said, \"Buddy, if you took that twelve hundred bucks and put it in a basic S&P 500 index fund, with compound interest over thirty years...\" but kids these days just want the instant gratification. They don't think long-term at all. \n\nIt's exactly the same with the younger advisors at my firm, honestly. They all want to push crypto and high-risk tech stocks because they think they're going to beat the market overnight. Or even when I do go to those gallery events with my wife, I'll realize I'm just standing there comparing my jaw to the other guys in the room instead of actually focusing on whatever conversation I'm supposed to be having. \n\nAnd then, yeah, I'll find myself messing with my shirt collar, or tilting my head to find a better angle. I even adjust the blinds in my office to change the lighting before someone walks in. Just trying to make sure it doesn't cast a weird shadow on my jaw, you know?\n\nDoctor: How much time per day would you say you spend thinking about the appearance of your jaw or checking your reflection because of it?\n\nPatient: I don't know, maybe... I mean, a rough estimate... I guess if you force me to put a number on it, maybe... I don't know, twenty minutes? Thirty? It's hard to say because it's not all at once. It's like, two minutes here in the bathroom, a minute there adjusting the blinds...

They went with this cheap supplier out of Ohio instead of the premium commercial grade stuff. I told him, "Greg, you're spending more on man-hours fixing these things than you saved on the initial purchase order." It's classic short-term thinking. But yeah, I don't really have a solid number for you. Just... bits and pieces of time throughout the day. I really haven't kept track.

Miss work? No, no, I still get through my work. I mean, my clients rely on me, I can't just not show up. Though... I will admit, I have been a bit slower getting out the door for client meetings lately. Just taking extra time to check the mirror, make sure the lighting isn't hitting my face weird. 

It actually made me late to see one of my oldest clients, Richard, last Tuesday. Richard made an absolute fortune in the lumber industry back in the nineties—fascinating guy, really. He was telling me about how the price of timber is fluctuating right now because of the housing market slowdown, which is entirely predictable if you look at the current mortgage rates and the Fed's reluctance to cut them. I keep telling my clients that real estate is going to be sluggish for at least another eighteen months, but nobody wants to listen to the boring, pragmatic advice... 

Wait, what were we talking about? Oh, my routine. Right. Yeah, so I'm getting out the door slower, and honestly, I’ve been a bit less present during some of those appointments, too. Just distracted, I guess. 

And as for social situations... well, I have skipped a few of those art openings with my wife recently. I just really didn't want to be seen, or worse, photographed. You know how they always have those gallery photographers snapping candids for their Instagram pages. And speaking of Instagram, the monetization models these platforms are using now are just completely unsustainable. They rely entirely on ad revenue from companies that are already slashing their marketing budgets for Q3. It's a tech bubble, plain and simple, just like 2001. 

But, uh... yeah. I haven't missed work, but I have skipped a few art shows and been a little distracted.

They put in these harsh LEDs last year to save on energy costs. I actually ran the numbers for the building manager—because I couldn't help myself—and I showed him that the payback period on those specific fixtures is like twelve years. By the time they actually see a return on investment, the technology will be obsolete anyway. Plus, everyone looks like a ghost under them. It's just bad business. If a client comes in to hand over half a million dollars for you to manage, they want to sit in a room that feels warm and secure, not like a hospital cafeteria, right? \n\nBut yeah, the jaw thing. It's definitely making me a little more hesitant socially. My wife loves going to these local art gallery openings—she's really into watercolors right now—and I've actually skipped the last few. I just told her I had to catch up on some quarterly reports, but honestly, I just didn't want to be seen. And I definitely didn't want to end up in the background of anyone's photos. Those gallery people are always taking pictures for their Instagrams, and I just... I didn't want to deal with seeing my face like that again.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel significant anxiety or distress when you think that others might notice the appearance of your jaw?\n\nPatient: I don't know if I'd call it significant anxiety. I mean, I guess I get a little worked up about it, but it's not like a panic attack or anything. It's more just... a really uncomfortable feeling, like a tightness in my chest when I think someone is staring right at the lopsided part. \n\nActually, speaking of tightness in the chest, I had this client last month who thought he was having a heart attack right in my office. Turned out it was just severe heartburn from this spicy food truck he went to for lunch. Those food trucks are everywhere now, especially downtown near the financial district. They charge like eighteen dollars for a taco plate, which is just robbery when you consider their overhead is basically gas and a parking permit. I was talking to one of the owners once, and he was telling me about his profit margins, and I was just floored. I mean, if you manage your supply chain right, a food truck is basically a license to print money. But anyway, my client was fine, just needed an antacid. \n\nBut yeah, the anxiety... I don't know if it's significant. I just don't like the idea of people staring at my jaw and thinking it looks weird. It's just unpleasant, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain behaviors, like checking your reflection, adjusting your posture, or seeking reassurance about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I suppose I do a bit of that. Like, right before a client comes in, I'll definitely head to the bathroom mirror, or I'll pull out my phone camera and check my reflection a few times. \n\nThe cameras on these new phones are just ridiculous, by the way. They have, what, three lenses on the back now? My nephew was trying to explain the optical zoom to me at Thanksgiving, but honestly, who needs that much resolution? He just bought the newest model, which is wild to me because he's still paying off his student loans at like seven percent interest. I tried to tell him, I said, \"Buddy, if you took that twelve hundred bucks and put it in a basic S&P 500 index fund, with compound interest over thirty years...\" but kids these days just want the instant gratification. They don't think long-term at all. \n\nIt's exactly the same with the younger advisors at my firm, honestly. They all want to push crypto and high-risk tech stocks because they think they're going to beat the market overnight. Or even when I do go to those gallery events with my wife, I'll realize I'm just standing there comparing my jaw to the other guys in the room instead of actually focusing on whatever conversation I'm supposed to be having. \n\nAnd then, yeah, I'll find myself messing with my shirt collar, or tilting my head to find a better angle. I even adjust the blinds in my office to change the lighting before someone walks in. Just trying to make sure it doesn't cast a weird shadow on my jaw, you know?\n\nDoctor: How much time per day would you say you spend thinking about the appearance of your jaw or checking your reflection because of it?\n\nPatient: I don't know, maybe... I haven't really timed it or anything. It's not like I'm sitting there with a stopwatch. Although, speaking of time, I did just get this new smartwatch. My wife bought it for my birthday because she said I needed to track my steps, but honestly, the battery life is terrible. You have to charge it every single night, which just seems like a massive design flaw. Yeah, I don't know exactly. It's just sort of... throughout the day, you know? A little bit here, a little bit there. I haven't really added it all up.\n\nDoctor: Can you give me a rough estimate of how many minutes a day you spend thinking about your jaw's appearance or checking your reflection because of it?\n\nPatient: I mean, a rough estimate... It's like, two minutes here in the bathroom, a minute there adjusting the blinds... \n\nSpeaking of the blinds, I actually had to have maintenance come up to my office three times last week because the mechanism on the left side kept sticking. The building manager, Greg, he's this guy who used to work in commercial real estate before the 2008 crash, and he was telling me how they cut corners on the window treatments when they renovated the floor. They went with this cheap supplier out of Ohio instead of the premium commercial grade stuff. Just... bits and pieces of time throughout the day. I really haven't kept track.\n\nDoctor: Has this concern about your jaw's appearance ever caused you to avoid social situations, miss work, or significantly change your daily routine?\n\nPatient: Miss work? Just taking extra time to check the mirror, make sure the lighting isn't hitting my face weird. \n\nIt actually made me late to see one of my oldest clients, Richard, last Tuesday. Richard made an absolute fortune in the lumber industry back in the nineties—fascinating guy, really. A scale from one to ten... I don't know, I'm not really sure how to quantify that. 

You know, whenever people ask me to put a number on a feeling, it always reminds me of those risk tolerance questionnaires I have my new clients fill out. Have you ever seen one of those? It's supposed to gauge how they'll handle market volatility, asking them to rate their comfort level with potential losses on a scale from one to ten. But honestly, self-reported metrics are notoriously unreliable in finance. A guy will circle an eight or a nine, telling me he's totally fine with aggressive growth and high risk, but the second the S&P drops even two percent, he's calling my office in an absolute panic wanting to move his entire portfolio into municipal bonds. It's just human nature, right? People don't actually know how to accurately score their own feelings until they're looking at a quarterly statement in the red. 

But, uh... anyway. You were asking about the distress. Yeah, I really couldn't give you an exact number. It bothers me, obviously, but I don't really know where it would fall on a scale like that.

My wife bought it for my birthday because she said I needed to track my steps, but honestly, the battery life is terrible. You have to charge it every single night, which just seems like a massive design flaw. I was reading an article the other day about how Apple and Garmin are basically in an arms race over battery technology, but neither of them wants to compromise on the screen brightness. It's fascinating from an investment standpoint, really. If you look at the supply chain for the lithium-ion batteries... \n\nBut anyway, what were you asking? Oh, how much time. Yeah, I don't know exactly. It's just sort of... throughout the day, you know? A little bit here, a little bit there. I guess if you force me to put a number on it, maybe... I don't know, twenty minutes? Thirty? It's hard to say because it's not all at once. It's like, two minutes here in the bathroom, a minute there adjusting the blinds... \n\nSpeaking of the blinds, I actually had to have maintenance come up to my office three times last week because the mechanism on the left side kept sticking. The building manager, Greg, he's this guy who used to work in commercial real estate before the 2008 crash, and he was telling me how they cut corners on the window treatments when they renovated the floor. They went with this cheap supplier out of Ohio instead of the premium commercial grade stuff. I told him, \"Greg, you're spending more on man-hours fixing these things than you saved on the initial purchase order.\" It's classic short-term thinking. You see it all the time with mid-cap companies trying to juice their quarterly earnings by slashing operational expenses, and it always bites them in the long run. \n\nBut yeah, I don't really have a solid number for you. Just... bits and pieces of time throughout the day. I really haven't kept track.\n\nDoctor: Has this concern about your jaw's appearance ever caused you to avoid social situations, miss work, or significantly change your daily routine?\n\nPatient: Miss work? But honestly, self-reported metrics are notoriously unreliable in finance. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I do. I mean, especially before client meetings. I'll go into the bathroom, check the mirror, then I pull out my phone camera and check that, too. I'll adjust the lighting if I can, maybe mess with my shirt collar, try to angle my face so the jawline doesn't look so... off. 

And then, even when I'm actually in the meeting, or at one of those gallery events with my wife, I catch myself just staring at other people. I'll look at the younger advisors at my firm, or just random guys at the art shows, and I'm just comparing my face to theirs. Which is ridiculous, because I should be focusing on the conversation. 

Like I was talking to this guy, a junior analyst we just hired out of Wharton. Smart kid, but his understanding of municipal bonds is entirely theoretical. They teach these kids all these complex modeling techniques, stochastic calculus, Monte Carlo simulations, but they have absolutely no idea how human psychology impacts market liquidity. I tried explaining to him that when interest rates spike, retail investors panic-sell their bond funds, which forces the fund managers to liquidate their highest quality paper just to meet redemptions, and suddenly you have a massive pricing dislocation in the secondary market. But he just kept talking about duration matching like it was some magic bullet. It’s exactly why algorithmic trading models always blow up during black swan events—they can't price in irrational human terror. 

But anyway, yeah. I was looking at his jaw the whole time he was talking about yield curves. So, yes, I guess I do a lot of checking and comparing.

I didn't want to deal with seeing my face like that again.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel significant anxiety or distress when you think that others might notice the appearance of your jaw?\n\nPatient: I don't know if I'd call it significant anxiety. I mean, I guess I get a little worked up about it, but it's not like a panic attack or anything. It's more just... a really uncomfortable feeling, like a tightness in my chest when I think someone is staring right at the lopsided part. \n\nActually, speaking of tightness in the chest, I had this client last month who thought he was having a heart attack right in my office. Turned out it was just severe heartburn from this spicy food truck he went to for lunch. Those food trucks are everywhere now, especially downtown near the financial district. They charge like eighteen dollars for a taco plate, which is just robbery when you consider their overhead is basically gas and a parking permit. I was talking to one of the owners once, and he was telling me about his profit margins, and I was just floored. I mean, if you manage your supply chain right, a food truck is basically a license to print money. But anyway, my client was fine, just needed an antacid. \n\nBut yeah, the anxiety... I don't know if it's significant. I just don't like the idea of people staring at my jaw and thinking it looks weird. It's just unpleasant, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain behaviors, like checking your reflection, adjusting your posture, or seeking reassurance about your appearance?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I suppose I do a bit of that. Like, right before a client comes in, I'll definitely head to the bathroom mirror, or I'll pull out my phone camera and check my reflection a few times. \n\nThe cameras on these new phones are just ridiculous, by the way. They have, what, three lenses on the back now? My nephew was trying to explain the optical zoom to me at Thanksgiving, but honestly, who needs that much resolution? He just bought the newest model, which is wild to me because he's still paying off his student loans at like seven percent interest. I tried to tell him, I said, \"Buddy, if you took that twelve hundred bucks and put it in a basic S&P 500 index fund, with compound interest over thirty years...\" but kids these days just want the instant gratification. They don't think long-term at all. \n\nIt's exactly the same with the younger advisors at my firm, honestly. They all want to push crypto and high-risk tech stocks because they think they're going to beat the market overnight. But... actually, speaking of the younger guys, I do catch myself looking at them a lot. Like, comparing my face to theirs. Or even when I do go to those gallery events with my wife, I'll realize I'm just standing there comparing my jaw to the other guys in the room instead of actually focusing on whatever conversation I'm supposed to be having. \n\nAnd then, yeah, I'll find myself messing with my shirt collar, or tilting my head to find a better angle. I even adjust the blinds in my office to change the lighting before someone walks in. Just trying to make sure it doesn't cast a weird shadow on my jaw, you know?\n\nDoctor: How much time per day would you say you spend thinking about the appearance of your jaw or checking your reflection because of it?\n\nPatient: I don't know, maybe... I haven't really timed it or anything. It's not like I'm sitting there with a stopwatch. Although, speaking of time, I did just get this new smartwatch. My wife bought it for my birthday because she said I needed to track my steps, but honestly, the battery life is terrible. You have to charge it every single night, which just seems like a massive design flaw. I was reading an article the other day about how Apple and Garmin are basically in an arms race over battery technology, but neither of them wants to compromise on the screen brightness. It's fascinating from an investment standpoint, really. If you look at the supply chain for the lithium-ion batteries... \n\nBut anyway, what were you asking? Oh, how much time. Yeah, I don't know exactly. It's just sort of... throughout the day, you know? A little bit here, a little bit there. I haven't really added it all up.\n\nDoctor: Can you give me a rough estimate of how many minutes a day you spend thinking about your jaw's appearance or checking your reflection because of it?\n\nPatient: I mean, a rough estimate... I guess if you force me to put a number on it, maybe... I don't know, twenty minutes? Thirty? It's hard to say because it's not all at once. It's like, two minutes here in the bathroom, a minute there adjusting the blinds... \n\nSpeaking of the blinds, I actually had to have maintenance come up to my office three times last week because the mechanism on the left side kept sticking. The building manager, Greg, he's this guy who used to work in commercial real estate before the 2008 crash, and he was telling me how they cut corners on the window treatments when they renovated the floor. They went with this cheap supplier out of Ohio instead of the premium commercial grade stuff. I told him, \"Greg, you're spending more on man-hours fixing these things than you saved on the initial purchase order.\" It's classic short-term thinking. You see it all the time with mid-cap companies trying to juice their quarterly earnings by slashing operational expenses, and it always bites them in the long run. \n\nBut yeah, I don't really have a solid number for you. Just... bits and pieces of time throughout the day. I really haven't kept track.\n\nDoctor: Has this concern about your jaw's appearance ever caused you to avoid social situations, miss work, or significantly change your daily routine?\n\nPatient: Miss work? No, no, I still get through my work. I mean, my clients rely on me, I can't just not show up. Though... I will admit, I have been a bit slower getting out the door for client meetings lately. Just taking extra time to check the mirror, make sure the lighting isn't hitting my face weird. \n\nIt actually made me late to see one of my oldest clients, Richard, last Tuesday. Richard made an absolute fortune in the lumber industry back in the nineties—fascinating guy, really. He was telling me about how the price of timber is fluctuating right now because of the housing market slowdown, which is entirely predictable if you look at the current mortgage rates and the Fed's reluctance to cut them. I keep telling my clients that real estate is going to be sluggish for at least another eighteen months, but nobody wants to listen to the boring, pragmatic advice... \n\nWait, what were we talking about? Oh, my routine. Right. Yeah, so I'm getting out the door slower, and honestly, I’ve been a bit less present during some of those appointments, too. Just distracted, I guess. \n\nAnd as for social situations... well, I have skipped a few of those art openings with my wife recently. I just really didn't want to be seen, or worse, photographed. You know how they always have those gallery photographers snapping candids for their Instagram pages. And speaking of Instagram, the monetization models these platforms are using now are just completely unsustainable. They rely entirely on ad revenue from companies that are already slashing their marketing budgets for Q3. It's a tech bubble, plain and simple, just like 2001. \n\nBut, uh... yeah. I haven't missed work, but I have skipped a few art shows and been a little distracted.\n\nDoctor: On a scale from 1 to 10, where 1 is not distressing at all and 10 is extremely distressing, how distressing is the thought that your jaw looks uneven?\n\nPatient: A scale from one to ten... I don't know, I'm not really sure how to quantify that. \n\nYou know, whenever people ask me to put a number on a feeling, it always reminds me of those risk tolerance questionnaires I have my new clients fill out. Have you ever seen one of those? It's supposed to gauge how they'll handle market volatility, asking them to rate their comfort level with potential losses on a scale from one to ten. But honestly, self-reported metrics are notoriously unreliable in finance. A guy will circle an eight or a nine, telling me he's totally fine with aggressive growth and high risk, but the second the S&P drops even two percent, he's calling my office in an absolute panic wanting to move his entire portfolio into municipal bonds. It's just human nature, right? People don't actually know how to accurately score their own feelings until they're looking at a quarterly statement in the red. \n\nBut, uh... anyway. You were asking about the distress. Yeah, I really couldn't give you an exact number. It bothers me, obviously, but I don't really know where it would fall on a scale like that.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that the concern about your jaw looking uneven causes you significant distress or interferes with your ability to function normally in social or work situations?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was saying a minute ago, I guess it does interfere a little, but I wouldn't say I'm not functioning. I mean, I still get through my work. I have to. But yeah, I have been slower getting out the door for client meetings, and honestly, I'm just less present during some of those appointments. My mind is just... elsewhere. People park their money in these abstract canvases thinking it's a brilliant hedge against inflation, but the liquidity is absolutely terrible. If you need to liquidate a piece like that during a recession, you are taking a massive haircut. I had a client try to use a modern sculpture as collateral for a bridge loan once, and the underwriters practically laughed him out of the room. Banks want hard assets, commercial real estate, cash equivalents... they don't care about a piece of welded scrap metal, no matter whose name is on the plaque. It's just basic risk management. \n\nBut... sorry, what was the question? Oh, right, the distress and functioning. Yeah, so, it's just like I said. I'm getting my work done, just... a bit distracted and avoiding those camera-heavy events.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel driven to perform certain behaviors or mental acts—like checking your reflection, comparing yourself to others, or adjusting your appearance—because of concerns about your jaw looking uneven?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I'll adjust the lighting if I can, maybe mess with my shirt collar, try to angle my face so the jawline doesn't look so... off. \n\nAnd then, even when I'm actually in the meeting, or at one of those gallery events with my wife, I catch myself just staring at other people. I tried explaining to him that when interest rates spike, retail investors panic-sell their bond funds, which forces the fund managers to liquidate their highest quality paper just to meet redemptions, and suddenly you have a massive pricing dislocation in the secondary market. But he just kept talking about duration matching like it was some magic bullet. It’s exactly why algorithmic trading models always blow up during black swan events—they can't price in irrational human terror. \n\nBut anyway, yeah. Uncontrollable? I don't know if I'd use that word. I mean, I guess I haven't really tried to stop, so I'm not sure if I could or not. It's just something I do. 

But you know, speaking of things being uncontrollable and excessive, it really reminds me of market sentiment lately. You literally cannot control it. Look at what happened with those meme stocks a while back. Completely irrational behavior, totally excessive, but retail investors just couldn't stop themselves. They were buying up shares of failing brick-and-mortar companies at astronomical valuations just because someone on an internet forum posted a rocket ship emoji. The short squeeze mechanics were fascinating to watch, sure, but from a wealth preservation standpoint, it was absolute madness. People just get fixated on something and they can't let it go, even when all the fundamentals tell them it's a terrible idea.

We were looking at his portfolio on Tuesday, and he is way too heavy in municipal bonds for a guy who's only forty-two. I told him, look, with inflation doing what it's doing right now, you're essentially just locking in a loss. You gotta have some growth in there or you'll be working until you're eighty. \n\nBut, uh... what were we talking about? Oh, right, my face. Yeah, sometimes my computer monitor goes to sleep and I'll catch my reflection in the black screen, and I'll just see that lopsidedness on my jaw. But I don't know exactly how much it bothers me overall. Just enough that I notice it and wish it looked normal, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if this concern about your jaw's appearance affects your mood, daily routine, or how you interact with others?\n\nPatient: Well, I mean, I still get through my work. I'm not calling in sick or anything. But I guess... I have been a little slower getting out the door in the mornings, especially if I have client meetings. You know, trying to make sure I'm angled right in the mirror before I leave. And then during the actual appointments, I feel like I'm maybe a little less present? Like I'm sitting there across from a client, and instead of fully focusing on their asset allocation, I'm wondering if the lighting in the conference room is highlighting the unevenness. \n\nOur office lighting is terrible, by the way. They put in these harsh LEDs last year to save on energy costs. I actually ran the numbers for the building manager—because I couldn't help myself—and I showed him that the payback period on those specific fixtures is like twelve years. By the time they actually see a return on investment, the technology will be obsolete anyway. Plus, everyone looks like a ghost under them. It's just bad business. If a client comes in to hand over half a million dollars for you to manage, they want to sit in a room that feels warm and secure, not like a hospital cafeteria, right? \n\nBut yeah, the jaw thing. It's definitely making me a little more hesitant socially. My wife loves going to these local art gallery openings—she's really into watercolors right now—and I've actually skipped the last few. I just told her I had to catch up on some quarterly reports, but honestly, I just didn't want to be seen. And I definitely didn't want to end up in the background of anyone's photos. Those gallery people are always taking pictures for their Instagrams, and I just... It's more just... a really uncomfortable feeling, like a tightness in my chest when I think someone is staring right at the lopsided part. \n\nActually, speaking of tightness in the chest, I had this client last month who thought he was having a heart attack right in my office. Turned out it was just severe heartburn from this spicy food truck he went to for lunch. Those food trucks are everywhere now, especially downtown near the financial district. They charge like eighteen dollars for a taco plate, which is just robbery when you consider their overhead is basically gas and a parking permit. I was talking to one of the owners once, and he was telling me about his profit margins, and I was just floored. He just bought the newest model, which is wild to me because he's still paying off his student loans at like seven percent interest. I even adjust the blinds in my office to change the lighting before someone walks in. It's like, two minutes here in the bathroom, a minute there adjusting the blinds... \n\nSpeaking of the blinds, I actually had to have maintenance come up to my office three times last week because the mechanism on the left side kept sticking. The building manager, Greg, he's this guy who used to work in commercial real estate before the 2008 crash, and he was telling me how they cut corners on the window treatments when they renovated the floor. They went with this cheap supplier out of Ohio instead of the premium commercial grade stuff. I told him, \"Greg, you're spending more on man-hours fixing these things than you saved on the initial purchase order.\" It's classic short-term thinking. Just taking extra time to check the mirror, make sure the lighting isn't hitting my face weird. \n\nIt actually made me late to see one of my oldest clients, Richard, last Tuesday. Richard made an absolute fortune in the lumber industry back in the nineties—fascinating guy, really. I haven't missed work, but I have skipped a few art shows and been a little distracted.\n\nDoctor: On a scale from 1 to 10, where 1 is not distressing at all and 10 is extremely distressing, how distressing is the thought that your jaw looks uneven?\n\nPatient: A scale from one to ten... I don't know, I'm not really sure how to quantify that. \n\nYou know, whenever people ask me to put a number on a feeling, it always reminds me of those risk tolerance questionnaires I have my new clients fill out. But honestly, self-reported metrics are notoriously unreliable in finance. A guy will circle an eight or a nine, telling me he's totally fine with aggressive growth and high risk, but the second the S&P drops even two percent, he's calling my office in an absolute panic wanting to move his entire portfolio into municipal bonds. I tried explaining to him that when interest rates spike, retail investors panic-sell their bond funds, which forces the fund managers to liquidate their highest quality paper just to meet redemptions, and suddenly you have a massive pricing dislocation in the secondary market. But he just kept talking about duration matching like it was some magic bullet. It’s exactly why algorithmic trading models always blow up during black swan events—they can't price in irrational human terror. \n\nBut anyway, yeah. I was looking at his jaw the whole time he was talking about yield curves. Didn't you just ask me that? Or something really similar? I feel like we just talked about this. But yeah, like I was saying earlier, I still get through my work. I have to. Plus, you're relying on a secondary market that is entirely dependent on discretionary income, which dries up the second the Fed raises rates. It's just a terrible risk-adjusted return. He just bought the newest model, which is wild to me because he's still paying off his student loans at like seven percent interest. I tried to tell him, I said, \"Buddy, if you took that twelve hundred bucks and put it in a basic S&P 500 index fund, with compound interest over thirty years...\" but kids these days just want the instant gratification. I even adjust the blinds in my office to change the lighting before someone walks in. Just trying to make sure it doesn't cast a weird shadow on my jaw, you know?\n\nDoctor: How much time per day would you say you spend thinking about the appearance of your jaw or checking your reflection because of it?\n\nPatient: I don't know, maybe... I haven't really timed it or anything. It's not like I'm sitting there with a stopwatch. Although, speaking of time, I did just get this new smartwatch. My wife bought it for my birthday because she said I needed to track my steps, but honestly, the battery life is terrible. You have to charge it every single night, which just seems like a massive design flaw. I guess if you force me to put a number on it, maybe... I don't know, twenty minutes? Thirty? It's hard to say because it's not all at once. It's like, two minutes here in the bathroom, a minute there adjusting the blinds... \n\nSpeaking of the blinds, I actually had to have maintenance come up to my office three times last week because the mechanism on the left side kept sticking. The building manager, Greg, he's this guy who used to work in commercial real estate before the 2008 crash, and he was telling me how they cut corners on the window treatments when they renovated the floor. They went with this cheap supplier out of Ohio instead of the premium commercial grade stuff. I told him, \"Greg, you're spending more on man-hours fixing these things than you saved on the initial purchase order.\" It's classic short-term thinking. You see it all the time with mid-cap companies trying to juice their quarterly earnings by slashing operational expenses, and it always bites them in the long run. \n\nBut yeah, I don't really have a solid number for you. Just... bits and pieces of time throughout the day. I really haven't kept track.\n\nDoctor: Has this concern about your jaw's appearance ever caused you to avoid social situations, miss work, or significantly change your daily routine?\n\nPatient: Miss work? No, no, I still get through my work. I mean, my clients rely on me, I can't just not show up. Though... I will admit, I have been a bit slower getting out the door for client meetings lately. Just taking extra time to check the mirror, make sure the lighting isn't hitting my face weird. \n\nIt actually made me late to see one of my oldest clients, Richard, last Tuesday. Richard made an absolute fortune in the lumber industry back in the nineties—fascinating guy, really. He was telling me about how the price of timber is fluctuating right now because of the housing market slowdown, which is entirely predictable if you look at the current mortgage rates and the Fed's reluctance to cut them. I keep telling my clients that real estate is going to be sluggish for at least another eighteen months, but nobody wants to listen to the boring, pragmatic advice... \n\nWait, what were we talking about? Oh, my routine. Right. Yeah, so I'm getting out the door slower, and honestly, I’ve been a bit less present during some of those appointments, too. Just distracted, I guess. \n\nAnd as for social situations... well, I have skipped a few of those art openings with my wife recently. I just really didn't want to be seen, or worse, photographed. You know how they always have those gallery photographers snapping candids for their Instagram pages. And speaking of Instagram, the monetization models these platforms are using now are just completely unsustainable. They rely entirely on ad revenue from companies that are already slashing their marketing budgets for Q3. It's a tech bubble, plain and simple, just like 2001. \n\nBut, uh... yeah. I haven't missed work, but I have skipped a few art shows and been a little distracted.\n\nDoctor: On a scale from 1 to 10, where 1 is not distressing at all and 10 is extremely distressing, how distressing is the thought that your jaw looks uneven?\n\nPatient: A scale from one to ten... I don't know, I'm not really sure how to quantify that. \n\nYou know, whenever people ask me to put a number on a feeling, it always reminds me of those risk tolerance questionnaires I have my new clients fill out. Have you ever seen one of those? It's supposed to gauge how they'll handle market volatility, asking them to rate their comfort level with potential losses on a scale from one to ten. But honestly, self-reported metrics are notoriously unreliable in finance. A guy will circle an eight or a nine, telling me he's totally fine with aggressive growth and high risk, but the second the S&P drops even two percent, he's calling my office in an absolute panic wanting to move his entire portfolio into municipal bonds. It's just human nature, right? People don't actually know how to accurately score their own feelings until they're looking at a quarterly statement in the red. \n\nBut, uh... anyway. You were asking about the distress. Yeah, I really couldn't give you an exact number. It bothers me, obviously, but I don't really know where it would fall on a scale like that.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that the concern about your jaw looking uneven causes you significant distress or interferes with your ability to function normally in social or work situations?\n\nPatient: Well, like I was saying a minute ago, I guess it does interfere a little, but I wouldn't say I'm not functioning. I mean, I still get through my work. I have to. But yeah, I have been slower getting out the door for client meetings, and honestly, I'm just less present during some of those appointments. My mind is just... elsewhere. And like I mentioned with my wife's art openings, I've skipped a few of those lately because I just really didn't want to be seen, or photographed. \n\nActually, speaking of those art openings, the gallery she goes to downtown is run by this guy Marcus. He used to be an appraiser, and let me tell you, the markup on contemporary art right now is completely unjustifiable. It's essentially an unregulated asset class. People park their money in these abstract canvases thinking it's a brilliant hedge against inflation, but the liquidity is absolutely terrible. If you need to liquidate a piece like that during a recession, you are taking a massive haircut. I had a client try to use a modern sculpture as collateral for a bridge loan once, and the underwriters practically laughed him out of the room. Banks want hard assets, commercial real estate, cash equivalents... they don't care about a piece of welded scrap metal, no matter whose name is on the plaque. It's just basic risk management. \n\nBut... sorry, what was the question? Oh, right, the distress and functioning. Yeah, so, it's just like I said. I'm getting my work done, just... a bit distracted and avoiding those camera-heavy events.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel driven to perform certain behaviors or mental acts—like checking your reflection, comparing yourself to others, or adjusting your appearance—because of concerns about your jaw looking uneven?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I mean, especially before client meetings. I'll go into the bathroom, check the mirror, then I pull out my phone camera and check that, too. I'll adjust the lighting if I can, maybe mess with my shirt collar, try to angle my face so the jawline doesn't look so... off. \n\nAnd then, even when I'm actually in the meeting, or at one of those gallery events with my wife, I catch myself just staring at other people. I'll look at the younger advisors at my firm, or just random guys at the art shows, and I'm just comparing my face to theirs. Which is ridiculous, because I should be focusing on the conversation. \n\nLike I was talking to this guy, a junior analyst we just hired out of Wharton. Smart kid, but his understanding of municipal bonds is entirely theoretical. They teach these kids all these complex modeling techniques, stochastic calculus, Monte Carlo simulations, but they have absolutely no idea how human psychology impacts market liquidity. I tried explaining to him that when interest rates spike, retail investors panic-sell their bond funds, which forces the fund managers to liquidate their highest quality paper just to meet redemptions, and suddenly you have a massive pricing dislocation in the secondary market. But he just kept talking about duration matching like it was some magic bullet. It’s exactly why algorithmic trading models always blow up during black swan events—they can't price in irrational human terror. \n\nBut anyway, yeah. I was looking at his jaw the whole time he was talking about yield curves. So, yes, I guess I do a lot of checking and comparing.\n\nDoctor: Do these behaviors or thoughts about your jaw ever feel uncontrollable or something you can't stop yourself from doing, even if you know they might be excessive?\n\nPatient: Uncontrollable? It's just something I do. \n\nBut you know, speaking of things being uncontrollable and excessive, it really reminds me of market sentiment lately. You literally cannot control it. Look at what happened with those meme stocks a while back. Completely irrational behavior, totally excessive, but retail investors just couldn't stop themselves. They were buying up shares of failing brick-and-mortar companies at astronomical valuations just because someone on an internet forum posted a rocket ship emoji. It completely defies the efficient market hypothesis. I had clients, smart people, calling me up demanding we allocate five percent of their retirement into a dying video game retailer. I told them they were trying to catch a falling knife, but it was like a feeding frenzy. Once the herd mentality takes over, logic just goes right out the window. The short squeeze mechanics were fascinating to watch, sure, but from a wealth preservation standpoint, it was absolute madness. People just get fixated on something and they can't let it go, even when all the fundamentals tell them it's a terrible idea. \n\nSo, uh... wait, what were you asking? Oh, if I can't stop myself from checking the mirror. Yeah, I really don't know. I just haven't really noticed if it's something I can't control, I just sort of do it without thinking about stopping.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your concern about your jaw's appearance causes you significant distress or makes it hard for you to function normally in your day-to-day life?\n\nPatient: Didn't you just ask me that? Or something really similar? I feel like we just talked about this. But yeah, like I was saying earlier, I still get through my work. I have to. I was talking to this guy at the last one I actually went to, and he was trying to convince me that buying fractional shares of classic cars through some new app was the future of alternative investing. I had to explain to him that the liquidity premium on a 1960s Ferrari is practically non-existent, and the carrying costs—insurance, specialized maintenance, climate-controlled storage—will eat up any potential capital appreciation before you even realize a gain. Plus, you're relying on a secondary market that is entirely dependent on discretionary income, which dries up the second the Fed raises rates. It's just a terrible risk-adjusted return. But he wouldn't listen. People just love the idea of owning a piece of a sports car they can't even drive. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, to answer your question again, the jaw thing does mess with me a bit. Just slower to meetings, distracted, skipping the camera-heavy stuff.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. People park their money in these abstract canvases thinking it's a brilliant hedge against inflation, but the liquidity is absolutely terrible. If you need to liquidate a piece like that during a recession, you are taking a massive haircut. I had a client try to use a modern sculpture as collateral for a bridge loan once, and the underwriters practically laughed him out of the room. Banks want hard assets, commercial real estate, cash equivalents... they don't care about a piece of welded scrap metal, no matter whose name is on the plaque. It's just basic risk management. \n\nBut... sorry, what was the question? Oh, right, the distress and functioning. Yeah, so, it's just like I said. I mean, especially before client meetings. I'll go into the bathroom, check the mirror, then I pull out my phone camera and check that, too. I'll adjust the lighting if I can, maybe mess with my shirt collar, try to angle my face so the jawline doesn't look so... off. \n\nAnd then, even when I'm actually in the meeting, or at one of those gallery events with my wife, I catch myself just staring at other people. I'll look at the younger advisors at my firm, or just random guys at the art shows, and I'm just comparing my face to theirs. Which is ridiculous, because I should be focusing on the conversation. \n\nLike I was talking to this guy, a junior analyst we just hired out of Wharton. Smart kid, but his understanding of municipal bonds is entirely theoretical. They teach these kids all these complex modeling techniques, stochastic calculus, Monte Carlo simulations, but they have absolutely no idea how human psychology impacts market liquidity. I tried explaining to him that when interest rates spike, retail investors panic-sell their bond funds, which forces the fund managers to liquidate their highest quality paper just to meet redemptions, and suddenly you have a massive pricing dislocation in the secondary market. But he just kept talking about duration matching like it was some magic bullet. It’s exactly why algorithmic trading models always blow up during black swan events—they can't price in irrational human terror. \n\nBut anyway, yeah. I was looking at his jaw the whole time he was talking about yield curves. They were buying up shares of failing brick-and-mortar companies at astronomical valuations just because someone on an internet forum posted a rocket ship emoji. It completely defies the efficient market hypothesis. I had clients, smart people, calling me up demanding we allocate five percent of their retirement into a dying video game retailer. I told them they were trying to catch a falling knife, but it was like a feeding frenzy. Once the herd mentality takes over, logic just goes right out the window. The short squeeze mechanics were fascinating to watch, sure, but from a wealth preservation standpoint, it was absolute madness. People just get fixated on something and they can't let it go, even when all the fundamentals tell them it's a terrible idea. \n\nSo, uh... wait, what were you asking? I just haven't really noticed if it's something I can't control, I just sort of do it without thinking about stopping.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your concern about your jaw's appearance causes you significant distress or makes it hard for you to function normally in your day-to-day life?\n\nPatient: Didn't you just ask me that? I had to explain to him that the liquidity premium on a 1960s Ferrari is practically non-existent, and the carrying costs—insurance, specialized maintenance, climate-controlled storage—will eat up any potential capital appreciation before you even realize a gain. Plus, you're relying on a secondary market that is entirely dependent on discretionary income, which dries up the second the Fed raises rates. It's just a terrible risk-adjusted return. Just slower to meetings, distracted, skipping the camera-heavy stuff.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your concern about your jaw's appearance causes you clinically significant distress or impairment in social, occupational, or other important areas of functioning?\n\nPatient: I swear we just went over this, right? ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, it's just my jaw. That's the only thing. I mean, my hairline isn't what it used to be, but I'm 45, you know? That's just depreciation. You expect it. 

Speaking of depreciation, though, it's amazing how many people don't understand how it works with real estate. I had this couple in my office last week, nice people, but they were convinced their rental property was just printing money because the rent covered the mortgage. I had to pull up the tax code and walk them through MACRS—Modified Accelerated Cost Recovery System. You've got 27.5 years to depreciate residential real estate, which is a fantastic tax shield if you use it right. But they weren't factoring in capital expenditures at all. You have to replace a roof or an HVAC system, that eats your cash flow for the whole year. And then when you go to sell, if you don't 1031 exchange it, depreciation recapture is going to hit you like a freight train. The IRS taxes that at 25 percent, not the standard long-term capital gains rate. People look at gross income and think they're real estate moguls, but they completely ignore the tax liabilities building up in the background. It's like looking at a gross return and ignoring the expense ratio on a mutual fund. 

But yeah, anyway, just the jaw. I don't really worry about the rest of my face or my body or anything like that. Just this one thing.

Banks want hard assets, commercial real estate, cash equivalents... they don't care about a piece of welded scrap metal, no matter whose name is on the plaque. It's just basic risk management. \n\nBut... sorry, what was the question? Oh, right, the distress and functioning. Yeah, so, it's just like I said. I'm getting my work done, just... a bit distracted and avoiding those camera-heavy events.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel driven to perform certain behaviors or mental acts—like checking your reflection, comparing yourself to others, or adjusting your appearance—because of concerns about your jaw looking uneven?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I mean, especially before client meetings. I'll go into the bathroom, check the mirror, then I pull out my phone camera and check that, too. I'll adjust the lighting if I can, maybe mess with my shirt collar, try to angle my face so the jawline doesn't look so... off. \n\nAnd then, even when I'm actually in the meeting, or at one of those gallery events with my wife, I catch myself just staring at other people. I'll look at the younger advisors at my firm, or just random guys at the art shows, and I'm just comparing my face to theirs. Which is ridiculous, because I should be focusing on the conversation. \n\nLike I was talking to this guy, a junior analyst we just hired out of Wharton. They teach these kids all these complex modeling techniques, stochastic calculus, Monte Carlo simulations, but they have absolutely no idea how human psychology impacts market liquidity. I tried explaining to him that when interest rates spike, retail investors panic-sell their bond funds, which forces the fund managers to liquidate their highest quality paper just to meet redemptions, and suddenly you have a massive pricing dislocation in the secondary market. But he just kept talking about duration matching like it was some magic bullet. It’s exactly why algorithmic trading models always blow up during black swan events—they can't price in irrational human terror. \n\nBut anyway, yeah. I was looking at his jaw the whole time he was talking about yield curves. So, yes, I guess I do a lot of checking and comparing.\n\nDoctor: Do these behaviors or thoughts about your jaw ever feel uncontrollable or something you can't stop yourself from doing, even if you know they might be excessive?\n\nPatient: Uncontrollable? I don't know if I'd use that word. I mean, I guess I haven't really tried to stop, so I'm not sure if I could or not. It's just something I do. \n\nBut you know, speaking of things being uncontrollable and excessive, it really reminds me of market sentiment lately. You literally cannot control it. Look at what happened with those meme stocks a while back. Completely irrational behavior, totally excessive, but retail investors just couldn't stop themselves. They were buying up shares of failing brick-and-mortar companies at astronomical valuations just because someone on an internet forum posted a rocket ship emoji. It completely defies the efficient market hypothesis. I had clients, smart people, calling me up demanding we allocate five percent of their retirement into a dying video game retailer. I told them they were trying to catch a falling knife, but it was like a feeding frenzy. Once the herd mentality takes over, logic just goes right out the window. The short squeeze mechanics were fascinating to watch, sure, but from a wealth preservation standpoint, it was absolute madness. People just get fixated on something and they can't let it go, even when all the fundamentals tell them it's a terrible idea. \n\nSo, uh... wait, what were you asking? But it does make me slower getting out the door for client meetings, and I’m definitely less present during some of those appointments. And I've skipped a few of my wife's art openings because I really just didn't want to be seen or photographed. \n\nWhich, honestly, skipping those art openings might be saving my sanity anyway. The people that show up to those things... it's just a giant echo chamber of pretentious wealth. I was talking to this guy at the last one I actually went to, and he was trying to convince me that buying fractional shares of classic cars through some new app was the future of alternative investing. I had to explain to him that the liquidity premium on a 1960s Ferrari is practically non-existent, and the carrying costs—insurance, specialized maintenance, climate-controlled storage—will eat up any potential capital appreciation before you even realize a gain. Plus, you're relying on a secondary market that is entirely dependent on discretionary income, which dries up the second the Fed raises rates. It's just a terrible risk-adjusted return. Just slower to meetings, distracted, skipping the camera-heavy stuff.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that your concern about your jaw's appearance causes you clinically significant distress or impairment in social, occupational, or other important areas of functioning?\n\nPatient: I swear we just went over this, right? Like, literally just a minute ago. But, yeah, I guess I can repeat it. It's... well, like I said, I still get through my work. I have to, obviously. But I'm definitely slower getting out the door for client meetings because I'm checking the mirror, and I'm just not as present during the appointments themselves. You look at a place serving high-end hors d'oeuvres like that, and you think they're raking it in, but the overhead is staggering. Food costs, labor, rent in a prime commercial district... it's a miracle any of them survive their first three years. I had a client once who wanted to liquidate a significant portion of his diversified portfolio to open a farm-to-table restaurant. I had to sit him down and show him the failure rates. I mean, you're taking capital that's earning a reliable, tax-advantaged 7% annualized and throwing it into a venture where the most likely outcome is a total loss of principal within 36 months. It's just bad capital allocation. I eventually talked him into a REIT that specialized in commercial dining spaces instead. Much safer play. \n\nBut anyway, yeah, the jaw thing. You expect it. \n\nSpeaking of depreciation, though, it's amazing how many people don't understand how it works with real estate. I had this couple in my office last week, nice people, but they were convinced their rental property was just printing money because the rent covered the mortgage. You have to replace a roof or an HVAC system, that eats your cash flow for the whole year. And then when you go to sell, if you don't 1031 exchange it, depreciation recapture is going to hit you like a freight train. The IRS taxes that at 25 percent, not the standard long-term capital gains rate. People look at gross income and think they're real estate moguls, but they completely ignore the tax liabilities building up in the background. It's like looking at a gross return and ignoring the expense ratio on a mutual fund. \n\nBut yeah, anyway, just the jaw. I don't really worry about the rest of my face or my body or anything like that. Just this one thing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Yeah, I suppose I do try to manage it. Before client meetings, I’ll usually check the bathroom mirror, or pull out my phone camera several times just to see how it looks. I might try to adjust the lighting in my office, maybe change my collar or angle my face a certain way so it doesn't look as bad. And I definitely catch myself comparing my face to the younger advisors at the firm. 

Which, honestly, these younger guys coming into the industry right now... it's terrifying. They pass their Series 7 and suddenly think they're the next Warren Buffett because they made a few bucks trading crypto on their phones in college. I had to pull one of our junior guys aside last week. He was actually considering pitching a Bitcoin ETF to a 68-year-old widow who needs steady, fixed-income yield to cover her living expenses. I had to explain to him what a fiduciary duty actually means. You don't put someone with a low risk tolerance and a short time horizon into a highly volatile asset class that's entirely dependent on market sentiment and regulatory whims. I mean, the SEC is just waiting to drop the hammer on half of these exchanges, and when they do, the liquidity will just vanish. It's reckless. They look at historical annualized returns of tech and crypto and completely ignore maximum drawdown risks. You have to preserve capital first. 

But, um... sorry, what were we talking about? Right, the jaw thing. Yeah, so I do the mirror checks and the collar adjustments. And then, like I said, even at those gallery events with my wife, instead of actually focusing on the conversation with whoever we're talking to, I just catch myself staring at other people, comparing my face to theirs.

I don't know, I'm not really sure how to quantify that. \n\nYou know, whenever people ask me to put a number on a feeling, it always reminds me of those risk tolerance questionnaires I have my new clients fill out. Have you ever seen one of those? It's supposed to gauge how they'll handle market volatility, asking them to rate their comfort level with potential losses on a scale from one to ten. But honestly, self-reported metrics are notoriously unreliable in finance. A guy will circle an eight or a nine, telling me he's totally fine with aggressive growth and high risk, but the second the S&P drops even two percent, he's calling my office in an absolute panic wanting to move his entire portfolio into municipal bonds.
